824. JAMES SAMUEL MILLS was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of January, 3 1/2 bushels of oats, value 10 s., the goods of Benjamin Smith, his master.
BENJAMIN SMITH . I am a coal-merchant, and live at Deptford—the prisoner was my carman. On the 21st of January, about six o'clock in the evening, I went to my foreman's counting-house, and took the key of the corn-bin, which is kept there—I put about a pint of canary-seed amongst the corn in the bin, I smoothed it over, and locked the bin—I hung the key in the usual place—I then went to my private counting-house, where my foreman was waiting to do our regular evening business—I staid there till half-past seven o'clock—the prisoner then came with the key of the granary in which the corn-bin is placed—as soon as he had hung up the key, I and my foreman went and opened the bin—I found a great hole in the corn, a quantity had been taken out, I thought about a sack—we locked the bin, looked about, and found a sack concealed under some straw and other rubbish, in a stable adjoining the stable where the prisoner keep his horses—I opened the sack, and found the corn, and the canary-seed mixed with it—I got the policeman, to whom I gave charge of the sack—I can swear to the corn from the canary-seed in the corn—I did not watch the key of the bin during that hour.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. The counting-house in which the key was, was open? A. Yes.
ALEXANDER MITCHELL . I saw the key of the bin frequently during the 21st of January—I believed it was remaining in the same place that evening, but it is evident the bin had been opened by somebody.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR ( police-constable R 202.) On the 21st of January, at half-past nine o'clock at night, I went to the stable where Mr. Smith pointed out the sack of corn, covered with straw and litter—there was some canary-seed amongst the corn—I watched the corn at night to see whether the prisoner would take it when he went out, but he did not, and when he came back, I took him to the counting-house—Mr. Smith asked him how that corn came there—he said he had saved it for about three weeks to give to the horses when they did a little harder work than usual—Mr. Smith said, "When did you put any into it last?"—he said, "There has been none put in since last Saturday."
Cross-examined. Q. Where was this conversation? A. In the counting-house—the sack was in the stable—I did not show it to the prisoner nor did Mr. Smith.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420131-825]
825. MARY GOSLING was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of January, 1 pair of boots, value 5 s., the goods of William Thomas.
WILLIAM THOMAS . I am a shoemaker, and live in Hare-street, Woolwich. About half-past two o'clock on the 27th of January, I was in the room adjoining my shop—I went out—the prisoner was in the shop, and was going out the door, she turned, and said, "Have you got any lasting boots that will fit me?"—I said, "I dare say I have"—I showed her some—she said, "Will you take a deposit, I will call on Saturday?"—I said, "Very well"—I showed her one or two pairs—and in the mean time I missed a pair of boots out of the window—as she was going down the steps, I perceived them under her arm—I brought her back, and sent for a policeman—I said, "I want these boots"—she said, "Don't give me into custody, I will give you double the value of the boots"—I had seen them safe about half-an-hour before.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you inquired as to the station and character of the prisoner? A. Yes, I find her family are respectable—I am very sorry to be here—I believe up this time she has borne a good character—she is married, and has one child at the breast.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 33.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor,
Confined Ten Days.
[trialAccount t18420131-826]
826. GEORGE DIXON, WILLIAM THOMPSON , and HENRY NORTON were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of January, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of a man, whose name name is unknown, from his person.
BENJAMIN LOVELL . About two o'clock on Friday, the 21st of January, I was on duty at Greenwich-hospital, and saw the prisoners in company with another person—there was a crowd as the King of Prussia was coming—I had seen the prisoners and the other before standing by themselves—I am sure they were acquainted—just as the band was coming out, the whole mob rushed up to the gate—I saw Dixon feeling a gentleman's pocket—I then directed a policeman to watch them—be went, and in eight or ten minutes they shifted their places, and I saw Norton take this handkerchief from a gentleman's pocket—Dixon and Thompson were then one on each side of him, close to him—he took it out with his left-hand, and had hold of the tail of the coat with his right—I was not able to find out who the person was—policeman No. 190 seized Norton, and Thompson got away—I caught him with my right hand with the handkerchief—Norton had handed the handkerchief to him—he held it up, and said, "Who has lost a handkerchief?"
Cross-examined by ME. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you within reach of the gentleman? A. No, one step would have brought me near enough to catch him—Evans was with me—I had a prisoner in each hand, which prevented my alarming the gentleman—we had a great difficulty to secure the prisoners—I did not follow the gentleman, nor ask him his name—I never had any cases before, of handkerchiefs from persons unknown—there were a good many persons there—I never saw Gray, the horse-keeper, till he came to the Magistrate on the Wednesday—he was not at the first examination—he came to the station the night before, and left his name there—I did not see him that night—he called at the station—I saw him at the police-office
on the Wednesday after—I spoke to him—I did not ask him how he came to leave his name at the station on the Sunday night—he came to give evidence, but I was not aware of it till the inspector told me—Gray said, "I have been here waiting for you."
Q. Did you say, "How did you come to know me?" A. Yes—no, I did not ask him in that way—I said, "Yes," but it was before you gave me the opportunity to know what I did say, and I contradicted it immediately—when Gray was at the station, he said, "I have been waiting for you, Mr. Lovell"—I suppose the inspector had told him my name—I said, "The inspector gave me a paper last night, your name is Gray, is it not?"—he said, "Yes"—I told him the parties would not be up from Maidstone till twelve o'clock—this was at the police-office—Evans and I had been at the hospital-gate from about ten in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Which gate was it? A. The eastgate—the one nearest to Woolwich—there were a good many people there.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You saw the three prisoners with another person, and you swear they were acquainted together? A. I have no doubt they were all acquainted—they were all together, and came to the gate together—I did not see them speak, but I will not say that they were not speaking—Evans pointed them out to me, and I looked over the road, and saw them—there was no one near them—they were on the other side of the road—there might be persons within ten or a dozen yards of them, or five or six yards—I swear there were not persons within two or three yards of them.
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190.) I was on duty, and saw the three prisoners and another in company with them, in front of the College-gates—they were standing by themselves, and several other persons standing in front of them, close to the gate, but I had seen them about an hour previous, and the four were then alone—there was a rush at the gate, and at that moment I suspected the robbery was committed—I collared Norton and Thompson—Thompson gave me the slip, but I secured Norton—Thompson was secured by Lovell, and Dixon by another officer—I saw Norton's hand a minute or two previous, and he had no handkerchief in it, but the minute 1 turned him round, he had a handkerchief in his hand, and it was passed over—Thompson took it out of his hand, held it up, and called out, "Who has lost a handkerchief?" and said he had picked one up—the prisoners resisted being taken—Dixon became violent, and threw himself down.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How near was Lovell to you at this time? A. Within two or three yards, I believe, at the furthest—I did not see the gentleman to whom it was said this handkerchief belonged—I saw a gentleman who I suspected had been robbed—I did not ask Lovell if he saw the gentleman that was robbed—I do not recollect that I did—I suspected it, but Lovell said he saw Norton take the handkerchief out of a gentleman's pocket—he did not point out the gentleman to me, but I knew he was very near me—Gray, the horse-keeper, works for Mr. Wheatley.
Q. Had you known Gray before that day? A. I never saw him—I cannot tell how he happened to go to the station—I knew nothing about him for two days afterwards—he was not at the first examination—he appeared at the second examination—some conversation took place at the
White Hart public-house corner, where the coaches stand, between Gray and some of our men—I heard that in conversation between the. men on duty—I told Lovell of it, but I believe he knew it before I did—I think he did, but I am not positive—when I spoke to Lovell about it, he said he heard there was a man coming up.
Q. Did he tell you he heard that in consequence of the conversation at the White Hart? A. Yes—I cannot tell whether he told me that on the Monday or the Tuesday—it was one or the other, I believe.
Q. Are you sure Lovell told you the reason he expected the man to come up was, that he had heard a conversation with the men at the White Hart? A. Yes, that was the cause, to the best of my belief—I have no doubt about it—I have no doubt that I heard Lovell say he expected the man to come up in consequence of conversation between that man and the policeman at the White Hart—I saw Gray first at the station on the Wednesday—the day of the last examination—I asked him what he knew about the case—there was no one with me in particular—Lovell was there, but he did not hear me ask the question, that I know of—I do not know whether he was near enough to bear it, he might have heard it—I will not undertake to say whether he heard it—he might be a couple of yards from me, but if he was engaged in conversation, he might not be attending to me—I do not know whether he was in conversation.
Q. On the oath you have taken, did not Lovell join in the conversation with you and Gray? A. Not at that time—I do not recollect whether he did in the course of that morning—I cannot say either one way or the other—the handkerchief is of no great value—I did not see any initials on it—I do not believe there are any—I searched Norton myself—I found no handkerchief on him, but one round his neck—if he went out-with a handkerchief of his own, he might have lost it—he had not one in his hand one or two minutes before I took him, and when I took him he had one.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE Q. You pointed out the three prisoners to Lovell? A. Yes—I called his attention to them—they were standing together when I first saw them—when I pointed them out to Lovell, the four of them were standing together by the gate—I was within two yards of the gate myself—they were between me and the gate—they were more to the left of Lovell—there was only one man between me and them, and that was the fourth man—he was blowing a cigar in my face—there was a great mob behind me, but I was standing between the prisoners and those behind me—I was not quite in front of the crowd—part of the crowd was in front of me—I was right amongst the crowd—from me to the furthest prisoner there was not more than two yards-—the prisoners were closer on the crowd.
GEORGE GRAY . I am horse-keeper to Mr. Wheatley. On the 21st of January I was at the East-gate, at Greenwich-hospital, waiting for my master's horse—I saw the three prisoners standing together amongst a crowd of persons—I saw Norton have a handkerchief in his hand, and he passed it to the other—I did not see where Norton took it from, but I saw it in his hand.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long have you known these policemen? A. About six months, both Lovell and Evans.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420131-827]
827. HENRY GRIMSHALL was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of January. 10 yards of printed cotton, value 10 s.; 1 shawl, value 5 s.; 3 gowns, value 1 l.; 2 yards of calico, value 1 s. 6 d.; 2 yards of linen cloth, value 6 s.; 12 shirt collars, value 6 s.; 2 towels, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 tobacco-pipe, value 5 s.; the goods of Harriet Burton.
HARRIET BURTON . I live at Deptford, and am a laundress; the prisoner lodged in my house. On the 26th of January I observed him going from the house about half-past ten o'clock, and asked him what he had got round his body—he refused to show me—I felt very much agitated, and would insist on seeing—I pulled open his jacket, and saw my gownpiece between his skin and shirt—I said, "For God's sake, what have you done, tell me? this is my gown, but if that is all I will let you go, if there is no more"—he said, "God strike me dead if I have any thing else"—I turned him round, and found this black silk shawl on him—I then looked on my stairs—he had broken my bed-room door open, and taken some things from there, and the room he slept in he had stripped—I have found nothing but these two articles—the others are lost—he robbed me of about 9 l. worth of things.
JOHN ROSCOE ( police-sergeant R 9.) I took the prisoner—he said he had taken three gowns more, and had sold them for 3 s. to a Jew.
Prisoner. This is all I did take besides the three gowns; the other things I know nothing about—I did it out of distress, I was out of work.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420131-828]
828. WILLIAM LIVERMORE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of January, 1 brooch, value 6 d.; 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 ring, value 10 s.; 23 sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 5 shillings; the property of Thomas Lewis.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL HAWGOOD . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Old Kent-road. I have a watch, ring, and brooch, pawned with me, on the 12th of January, by the prisoner—I am quite certain I took the watch, ring, and brooch from him—I asked how much he wanted to raise—he said 1 l. or 2 l.—he then told me he had been out on a spree all night, and had spent all his money—I looked at the seal, the brooch, and ring, and said, "What do you want?"—he said, "Lend me 2 l."—I said I could not, but would lend him 25 s.—he said that would not be enough—I said, "I will make it 30 s."—while I was making the duplicate he said, "You may perhaps not be aware who you are serving"—I said, "No"—he said, "Then I will tell you; I am the noted Lord Waldegrave"—I presented him the duplicate—he said, "No, I will not take the duplicate, but I will give you my signature, and when I send a note with that signature, you can deliver it to the party"—he said his income was 8000/. a year—no one ever came with a signature like that he left—this is the signature he left ( producing a paper.)
ELIZABETH HENDERSON I am a widow, residing in Canterbury-road, Greenwich. In the beginning of December I became acquainted with the prisoner—I was then living in Church-street, Greenwich—my brother lives at Woolwich—I had a trunk at that time in my possession belonging to him—I kept the trunk and my brother kept the key—the prisoner represented himself to me as Lieutenant Lewis, of the Navy—he said he was on half-pay, and should receive it on the 10th of January—he made overtures of marriage to me, and we were to have been married the Wednesday after
he was taken—I believe the banns were put up while I was living in Church-street, Greenwich—he dined with me and my brother there—he borrowed 5 l. of my brother, and gave him a note to pay him 6l. for it on the 10th of January—my brother took the money out of the trunk in presence of the prisoner—the next day I moved to Peckham—the prisoner went before me, and took my brother's trunk with him—it was put up stairs in a spare room, at Peckham—one Sunday the prisoner gave me a sovereign, and told roe to make it last to keep house till the 10th of January—the prisoner and my brother went up stairs together, and there my brother gave him the sovereign—he gave it to me—I remember one day the prisoner brought a young man home with him—he said it was his brother James, and he had given him 10 l. that day, as he had bought two bear-skin coats—he brought the coats home with him—he told me that he took them out of pawn at Greenwich—he had u boa on then which he had not had before—the prisoner dined with me and my brother on the day my brother missed hit money, and after dinner the prisoner said to my brother, "We will go oat this afternoon; you put on one of these coats"—my brother said, "If I put on one of these coats, I shall go up stairs and pat on another pair of trowsers"—he went up, and then came down"—I said to my brother,"Do you know what you promised to give this man?"—he said, "Yes, a ring, I had forgot it"—my brother went up stairs to get it—he came down and said his money, ring, and watch were gone—the prisoner said, "Take this coat, and say nothing about it"—I said, "You villain, you have robbed my brother"—I did not authorise him to pledge the ring or watch.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not sleep with you the first night I saw you? A. Yes, and four nights after—I did not tell you I had been to Woolwich to see a marine who had seduced me, nor that I was on the town previous to my marriage with Henderson—I know a person named Boyle, and lived with him—I was helper at Greenwich College—I was dismissed because I loved a drop of drink—the watch belonged to Boyle, but it was mine after his death—I gave it to my brother, for fear it should be lost, and he held it as security for what I owed him—I came home and told you I had received the money due to Henderson a? prize-money, but I never received 1 s. due to him since I have been acquainted with you—we dined with Thomas on the Sunday after he dined with us—I went to Mrs. Wheeler's, and paid her 1 l. 4 s. you owed her, out of the 5 l. you borrowed of my brother—I was not asked in Chatham church to this marine—you did not meet me as a common prostitute in the street—I never told my brother I had got all my things out of pawn, and had five new caps—we lived by my pledging—I never considered you as a lodger.
MR. HORRY. Q. Had any body the keys of the spare room but the prisoner? A. Not a soul—my things were pawned, at his suggestion, to support him.
THOMAS LEWIS . I am a labourer, living at Woolwich. Elizabeth Henderson is my sister—she had the care of a trunk of mine for seven or eight years—it contained twenty-nine sovereigns, five shillings, two half-crowns, this watch, ring, and brooch—they were all in my trunk—on the 29th of December I was at my sister's house—I on that occasion lent the prisoner five sovereigns—he gave me a paper for 6 l.—on the Sunday following I dined at my sister's again—after the 29th of January my sister removed to Peckham, and there she and the prisoner and I dined together—my box was up stairs—something was said about my lending him a
sovereign—I went up stairs to my box—the prisoner came up after me—he saw my box when it was unlocked—the sovereigns were in a bit of paper under the till—I opened the paper, and took a sovereign out—he went down stairs—I locked up the box, and went down—at that time my watch, brooch, ring, and twenty-three sovereigns were all safe—I kept the key of my box in my pocket—on the 12th of January I was there—I had promised the prisoner a gold ring—I went up stairs to my box to fetch the ring, and left the prisoner below—the property was then all gone—among it the paper given me by the prisoner—I said I would get a policeman, and have him taken—I said, "I have been robbed"—he said, "You bad better take this coat, and make it all right"—he said, "Do you think it is me?"—I said, "I don't know"—I went out directly, and he went out with me—he said, "Are you going to have me taken?"—I said, "I am, when I can find a policeman"—he said, "You walk this way, I will walk that"—he walked away—I did not see him again till he was at Union-hall—I did not authorise him to pledge the watch, brooch, or ring.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not ask you for the sovereign up stairs, and did you not bring it down? A. No, I gave it you up stairs.
Prisoner's Defence. I can assure you that though I appear guilty, I am not the thief; the woman is a prostitute; after I lived with her at Greenwich I took a cottage at Peckham; I had not money to pay the rent; she proposed we should borrow it from Lewis; I asked him, and he lent me 5 l.; I pawned the watch, but it was given me to pawn; it belonged to Boyle, whom she lived with for three years.
GUILTY *. Aged 40.— Transported for Seven Years.

829. JAMES KING was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of January, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of Robert Butcher, from his person.
ROBERT BUTCHER . I live in Collingwood-street, Blackfriars. I was standing near Greenwich Hospital on the 22nd of January—I received information—I felt, and my handkerchief was gone—this is my handkerchief—it was safe before I went to Greenwich.
GEORGE JOB ( police-constable R 174.) About twenty minutes before two o'clock, I was on duty, and saw the prisoner go behind the prosecutor, with two others—one of them took the handkerchief out of the prosecutor's pocket, and passed it behind him—the prisoner took it—I collared him, and put my hand into his pocket, and took the handkerchief out—I took the person who took it, but he escaped—they had been together for about ten minutes.
Prisoner's Defence. I went down to see the King of Prussia land; two young men came up, and one of them had the handkerchief; he threw it down; I picked it up, and put it into my pocket.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.

[trialAccount t18420131-831]
831. JOHN M'GREGOR was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of January, 1 mare, price 30 l., the property of James Edwards.
STEPHEN SIMPSON . I am in the service of James Edwards, a coach proprietor, of Rochester, in Kent. He has stables in different parts of Kent—I have seen a bay mare in possession of a policeman at Kennnington, in the parish of Lambeth—it was Mr. Edwards's property, and worth 30 l.—I had seen the same mare at Dartford last Saturday week, the 22nd of January, in my master's stables—it had been moved from Dartford to North-fleet.
JOSEPH CLARKE ( police-constable L 157.) I vent to Mr. King's liverystable, in Queen's-row, Kennington, on Thursday, the 27th of January—I saw the prisoner there with Mr. King, standing in a two-stall stable, by the side of this bay mare, which is the same Simpson claims as Mr. Edwards's property—I asked the prisoner if the mare belonged to him—he said, "Yes"—I asked where he brought her from—he said from Portsmouth, and that he had brought her that morning from Croydon—I told him I supposed it to be stolen, and I should take him into custody on my own responsibility—he said he would go any where with me—I said I supposed he would—we left the stable, and went about 200 yards—he then said, "Stop"—I said, "Mind, whatever you say must be given in evidence against you, if you say any thing—I don't want to hear any thing"—be said, "No, I will tell you the truth, I stole the mare from Northfleet;" that he got into the stable by a-back way, and laid down about two hours; that he then got up, felt about the different horses, and picked this one out—I asked if he was not afraid of getting kicked—he said, "No," and that he found a pickaxe, and broke open the door the horse usually go in at, and took the mare away.
JOHN KING . I am proprietor of a livery-stable in Queen's-row, Lambeth. I saw the prisoner in my stable-yard on the 26th of January, about four o'clock in the afternoon, with the mare—he asked if I would purchase the mare—I asked what he wanted for her—she was brought out of one stable into another—he asked 12 l. for her—I said I should not buy her—I went away, leaving him there—I saw no more of him till twelve o'clock at night—I gave information to Clarke next morning.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not ask if you knew anybody who wanted a mare of that description? A. No—I did not say, "Wait till Harry Balls comes over, and if he does not buy her I will"—I said, "Wait, and very likely Balls will come over to look after her"—and in the meantime I went out to Clarke, to give him information as I supposed it was stolen—I gave information the next day, as I had a job to go with a fly and yon went away—I did not see you again till next morning, and then I went and fetched Clarke.
Prisoner. This man and his partner have been receivers of stolen property for several years. Had it not been for his partner, the mare never would have been there—a year and a half ago his partner received property to a considerable amount. Witness. The person is not my partner at all—he is my brother.
Prisoner. I know people who have taken things to him, and he has received them from them; and they have been advertised in the newspapers
for months. I should say one-third of his property does not belong to him lawfully.
THOMAS RUSSELL . I am a horse-keeper to James Edwards, at Northfleet, which is five miles from Dartford. On Wednesday morning, the 26th of January, I was at the stable at Northfleet—I found the stable-door broken into three pieces, and the mare gone, which I had locked up over night—the pickaxe was moved from over the corn-bin, where I had left it over night—there is a pig-pound adjoining the stable, and the outside boarding of that pound was pulled away—the mare was brought again to Northfleet on Saturday night—I know the prisoner—he was in Mr. Edwards's service, and when he left I succeeded to his situation.
STEPHEN SIMPSON re-examined. I have not seen the mare at Northfleet since—the man is not here who brought her back.
JOSEPH CLARKE re-examined. I sent her to Northfleet by a very respectable man who usually takes horses for Mr. Edwards.
GUILTY . Aged 33.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420131-832]
832. FREDERICK CLARK was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of January, 214lbs. weight of coals, value 2 s., the goods of Charles Hoppe and another, in a barge on the navigable river Thames.
HENRY JOHN KING ( Thames police-constable, No 30.) On the morning of the 10th of January I saw the prisoner in a boat alongside the Bull coal-barge, belonging to Mr. Hoppe, which was lying in the Thames by the Surrey Canal—I saw him take both his arms and sweep a quantity of coals from the bulk of the barge, into his boat—he then rowed away some distance down the river, then rowed back again, and threw his drag overboard—I rowed after him, took him into custody, and found the coals in his boat—he said he had dragged them up—there were 214lbs. weight.
Prisoner. I got the coals by the Surrey-canal, where there were plenty of men by. I did not take them out of the barge. Witness. I saw him take them.
JOSEPH PARRY . I am lighterman to Mr. Charles Hoppe and another. The officer told me of this—I went to the barge, and perceived that a few coals bad been taken out—those produced by the officer correspond with the coals in the barge.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18420131-833]
833. JOSEPH PAYNE was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Thomas Webb, on the 10th of January, at St. George the Martyr, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 2 watches, value 2 l.; 2 watch-guards, value 1 s.; 10 spoons, value 10 s.; 1 scent-box, value 1 s.; 1 thimble, value 1 s.; 1 pair of earrings, value 2 s. 6 d.; 5 necklaces, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 brooch, value 1 s.; 1 purse, value 1 s.; 4 half-crowns, 7 shillings, 4 sixpences, 2 groats, 4 pence, 5 halfpence, and 1 farthing; his property.
MARY ELIZABETH WEBB . I am the wife of William Thomas Webb, a wire-drawer, at No. 64, Kent-street, in the parish of St. George the Martyr, Southwark—he keeps the house—I keep a shop in the general line. On the 10th of January, about ten minutes or a quarter after eight o'clock, I went out with my husband—I locked up every thing safe—we fastened the shop-door, and then the street-door, after us—the windows were all
closed—we returned in an hour and a quarter, and found several people about the house, and a policeman in the shop—I found the door of the shop wrenched from the hinges, and missed from a room adjoining the shop, from a drawer which I had locked, and which was broken open, two silver watches, six silver tea-spoons, three coral necklaces, a silver scent-box, a silver thimble, a brooch, a pair of gold-wire earrings, and several trifling articles—I cannot say how much money I missed, but I suppose from 20 s. to 25 s. from the same drawer, consisting of two half-crowns, sixpences, shillings, a few pence, and a sixpence with a hole in it—I had locked the drawer, and had the key in my pocket when I went out—the prisoner was then in custody.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Where does your landlord live? A. Four doors off from us—we rent the whole premises—I am certain I locked the door—there are lodgers in the house, but they get in at the street-door—it is a spring-lock, which fastens when you that it—the lodgers were in the house at the time—the shop-door was wrenched—I missed nothing from the shop—the prisoner had been in the house before this—I am certain of him.
THOMAS HAWKINS . I live at No. 35, Castle-street, near the prosecutor's. On the night of the 10th of January, about twenty minutes to nine o'clock, I had occasion to go up the passage in which their shop-door it, to speak to a shopman of mine—I was talking to him for about a quarter of an hour, and as I came down a ladder from him, I saw a light in Webb's back-parlour, which communicates with the shop—I looked in, and saw two men in the back-parlour—I had no suspicion, but when I came up the passage I saw the door of the house open—I caught hold of the shop-door, and called out three times for Mr. Webb—nobody answered—one man came to me out of the shop, from towards the parlour, and asked me for some name—I said, "I do not know such a name"—he ran away as hard as he could—I then had suspicion, and closed the door, pursued him a short distance, and, just as I returned back, I saw another man come out of the same door—I turned my head and saw Marshall, a policeman, and pointed out to him the man who I had seen come out of the door—I am sure I pointed out the same man as I had seen come out of the door.
Cross-examined. Q. How did you get into the house? A. I went in at the street-door, which was a-jar—I am in the habit of going to the house three or four times a day, and generally found the door a-jar, but the shop was shut up at this time—my shopman lodges in the back premises.
EDWARD MARSHALL . I am a policeman. Hawkins pointed out a person in the street to me—I pursued him, and did not lose sight of him—I secured him—It was the prisoner—I had received instructions about the prisoner at an earlier period of the evening, and was on the watch—I took him inside the shop, searched him, and found an iron crow-bar in his coat pocket—we handcuffed him, and took him to the station, and there found a screw-driver in his pocket—a skeleton key dropped from his person when we were putting the handcuffs on him in the shop—the sergeant took it up—-the door of the shop in the passage was forced off the hinges, which this instrument would enable a person to do—there were marks of one like it on the door—it appeared to have forced the door—the street door was a-jar—it must have been opened with a key—there was no marks of violence there—I found all this property on him at the station.
THOMAS WATKINS . I am a police-sergeant. I have known the prisoner some years—about twenty minutes or half-past eight o'clock I saw him near Webb's house with another man, and from what I observed, I desired Marshall to keep a look out—I came into the shop after he was taken—I saw a skeleton key fall from him, and took it up—after going to the station, I returned, and tried the key to the drawers—it locked it and opened it—the crow-bar would enable a person to get the shop door open.
Cross-examined. Q. You mean the shop door was broken open? A. Yes—I only found one key on the prisoner.
MRS. WEBB re-examined. Here are six silver spoons, two salt spoons, and all the property I lost is here—it is mine.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420131-834]
834. MICHAEL PINE was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Atkins, on the 22nd of January, at Lambeth, and stealing therein 1 coat, value 30 s.; 1 sheet, value 3 s.; 1 blanket, value 3 s.; 2 pillow-cases, value 45.; 1 counterpane, value 3 s.; and 1 petticoat, value 2 s.; his property.
CATHERINE ATKINS . I am the wife of Thomas Atkins, a lighterman, and live in John-street, Commercial-road, in the parish of Lambeth—we occupy the whole house. On Saturday, the 22nd of January, I went out about half-past five o'clock in the afternoon, leaving my little girl, twelve years old, down in the kitchen—I locked the parlour-door, and left the key with her in the kitchen—I shut the street-door, and put the shutters to, but did not fasten them—I came back about six, and found the shutters open, and the bottom sash of the window thrown right up, so as to enable any one to get in—it was shut down when I had gone out—I knocked, and my little girl came to the door—I went in and missed the articles stated—I saw them again about nine the same evening, at Tower-street station—they are worth about 2 l. 5 s.
JASPER MALLION ( police-constable L 21.) On the evening of the 22nd of January, about a quarter to six o'clock, I saw the prisoner in Cornwali-road Lambeth, about a hundred yards from the prosecutor's house, and coming in a direction from it—he had a bundle with him—I was about crossing over to him—he saw I was making up to him, and dropped the bundle, and walked on—I took him and the bundle—it contained this blanket, sheet, and counterpane, which I produce.
Prisoner. I had not got them—he took me about a hundred yards away from the things, which were lying on the ground. Witness. No; not above twelve yards—there was a female standing near him by a gate, but I saw him for seven or eight yards before I crossed over—I was on the opposite side of the way, and suspecting him, made towards him, and he dropped the bundle by his side, close by the gateway of a doctor's shop—he did not attempt to run away—I went and took him, and then returned and got the bundle.
Prisoner. He asked the woman if I had left them there, and she said I did not. Witness. I said to the woman, "You saw him drop these, of course," but she made no answer—I distinctly saw the bundle drop from the prisoner.
MRS. ATKINS re-examined. These are the things I lost (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 15.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Justice Cresswell.
[trialAccount t18420131-835]
835. CHARLES BROCK was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the counting-house of William Kitson and another, on the 19th of January, at St. George the Martyr, Southwark, and stealing therein 1 cash-box, value 30 s.; 1 pocket-book, value 2 s. 6 d.; I piece of parchment, value 1 s.; 12 sovereigns, 16 half-crowns, 80 shillings, 46 sixpences, 60 groats, 60 pence, 100 halfpence, and 15 l. bank-note; their property.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM KITSON . I am in partnership with Mr. Robinson, as soapboilers, in White-street, Borough—the prisoner was in our employ for rather better than a fortnight. On Wednesday the 19th of January, my cash-box contained twelve sovereigns, a 5 l. Bank of England note, 8 l. in silver, two 5 s. papers of coppers, and the lease of our premises—one packet of copper consisted of pence, and the other of halfpence, and the silver consisted principally, of shillings—I locked the cash-box up in my private desk in the counting-house, about half-past three o'clock, when I left—the prisoner was then at work behind the counting-house, only separated from me by a slight partition and glazed work, so that be could see into the counting-house, and I could see over the premises—he was melting fat at a copper exactly behind the partition, within about eighteen feet of it—he could see every thing that was going on in the counting-house—I went Co the premises about twelve the next morning, found my partner there, and received information of the robbery—I called all the men together—they were all on the spot except the prisoner, who I did not see again till he was in custody at Union Hall, on the Friday following.
RICHARD ROBINSON . I am partner with Mr. Kitson. I remember his leaving the premises on the Wednesday afternoon—I locked up the counting-house myself about half-past four o'clock—I locked the door quite safe—there is a window to the counting-house, which opens on hinges like a door—I will not say whether that was fastened, but it was closed, and it is generally fastened when it is closed—I then left the premises—the prisoner was then employed behind the counting-house—I could not see any one employed there besides him at that time—I went to the premises again between ten and eleven next morning—I found the counting-house door open—the lock had been forced back, the small window was also open, and Mr. Kitson's desk forced open, and the cash-box taken out—I had all the men brought down, and pointed it out to them—the prisoner was among them—I told them to take notice of it, and that I should make further inquiries when Mr. Kitson came, and that I expected him in a short time—the prisoner, when he saw it, held up his bands as if to express his surprise at the robbery—I sent them all to their work, and in about three quarters of an hour or an hour Mr. Kitson came—I told him the circumstance, and had the men called down again, but the prisoner could not be found—he had left the premises, and did not return—there were marks on the counting-house window, as if made by greasy fingers, and also marks outside and inside the door, as if some instrument had been applied to force it—it
appeared to me as if an attempt had been made to force it both from the outside and inside—it could be opened from the inside by forcing the lock back, but in no other way without the key—the window fastens with a sort of snap, which by tapping at the outside would fall down, and the window could then be opened.
Prisoner. Mr. Robinson says there was no other person working behind the counting-house but me that day, there was a smith and his boy attending to the boiler in the yard. Witness. I think there was, but not near the counting-house—the chimney of the copper was between them and the prisoner—they could not see into the counting-house from where they were working—they might when they passed by.
SAMUEL HARRIS ( police-constable L 177.) On Thursday, the 20th of January, about half-past one o'clock in the day, I was walking in the New Cut, which is no great distance from the prosecutor's premises, and saw the prisoner drunk—in consequence of something that had been told me, I went and took him into custody for being drunk and incapable of taking care of himself—I had not received information about the robbery then—I found on him six sovereigns, a half-sovereign, 3 s. in silver, one dozen and a half of spoons, one pepper-castor, and a cream jug, all metal and quite new—about three hours and a half afterwards, when he got better, he was asked in my presence how he came by this money—he said it was an annuity left him by his mother, and he had received at Cockburn's banking-house that day, the sum of 17 l.—he said he lodged at the house of Elizabeth Williams, No. 71, Granby-street—I went there that same night, and found a new blue pilot coat, a new suit of moleskin, some new flannel, a boa, some calico, three pairs of stockings, and a piece of toweling—I afterwards went to Mr. Lewis's shop, showed him the moleskin, and got from him this hat, round frock, and pair of trowsers—I produce all the articles—I was present when the prisoner was examined before the Magistrate—I told the Magistrate I had been to Cockburn's to make inquiry whether the prisoner had an annuity, and whether he received it on that day, and that they had told me no—the prisoner upon that remarked that he was drunk at the time he told me so—this cash-box and book I received from a stranger at Union Hall, on the 20th, who said he had picked them up.
ISAAC LEWIS . I live in the New Cut On Wednesday evening, the 19th of January, the prisoner came to my shop, about a quarter to eight o'clock, and bought a suit of white moleskin and a pilot coat, which came 30 s.—he paid me 19 s. 6 d. and a fourpenny-piece in silver, and four half-to and two 5 s. papers of coppers—one packet was halfpence and the other penny-pieces—on Thursday he came again and bought a hat, a pair of trowsers, and a round frock, for which he paid me 26 s. in silver and 3 d.—the silver consisted of shillings and sixpences—he did not take those things away with him—I had not a hat his size, and he said, "I will pay you for the hat, get me one to fit, and I will call for it to-morrow"—I afterwards gave the things up to the officer—the suit of moleskin produced is what I sold the prisoner—he had been drinking when he came on Thursday—he was sober on Wednesday.
WILLIAM ARMAN ( police-constable M 95.) On Thursday morning, the 20th of January, in consequence of information, I went to No. 71. Granby-street—I found nothing then—in the afternoon I went there again with Harris, and found this moleskin suit, and a number of new
things, which I produce—we took Williams, who was there, to the station—I have since received this bonnet from her.
ELIZABETH WILLIAMS . I live at No. 71, Granby-street, Lambeth. I have known the prisoner near upon a fortnight—on Wednesday evening, the 19th of January, between eight and nine o'clock, I met him near the Cornwall-road—he gave me a trifle of money that evening—I cannot swear how much, but I should say between 3 l. and 4 l. in silver—I saw he had some gold, but cannot say how much—I bought a new bonnet and a number of other things with the money, and some he brought himself—he had never given me such a sum of money before, not more than 5 s. or. 6 s. at the most—he had given me about that much on the Saturday evening—he brought the moleskin-dress, an 1 the pilot-coat himself—the silver he gave me was mostly in shillings, and there were about 2 s. worth of. fourpenny-pieces, and two or three half-crowns.
JOSEPH CROUCH . I am a carpenter, employed on the prosecutors' premises, and have a little workshop there. On Wednesday, the 19th of January, about half-past five or six o'clock, the prisoner came to my workshop, and asked for a chisel to ease a door—I asked what for, and said he would spoil any door on the premises—he took me to the foldingdoors, which are on the flooring of the soap-factory, in a line from my shop to the coppers, and said, "This is what I want to ease"—I told him if he did such a thing, he would injure the door, and I would not allow it—he then left me, and went to work at the coppers—in about half-an-hour I went down stairs to get a pail of soap-ashes, and found him against the counting-house without a light, between the copper and the counting-house—I asked why he was there without a light—he said he did not want one—I said it was very wrong his being there without a light, and then, left him—I had not been up stain above a quarter or half-an-hour, before he came up and wished me good night—that was about a quartet-past six—he then left, and never spoke to a soul there—I followed him with my light, but he shut the folding-doors, and I saw no more of him—the folding-doors are generally open till we all go away, that we may see persons come up the passage, but he shut them, which would prevent my seeing into the passage—we cannot see the counting-house from our door without coming outside—next morning I came to work just after seven, and found the position of two of my screw-drivers altered, placed different to what I had left them when I went away the night before—I went to the counting-house door and found marks on the desk and on the door, externally and internally, which might have been made by either of those screw-drivers—the door must have been opened from the inside—it could not be opened outside—it throws back—I know the counting-house window well—it is large enough for me to get through.
Prisoner's Defence. I never asked the man for any chisel; there are other men employed on the premises: on the night of the robbery I was off the premises before any of the men left—there was an excise man there the whole day, and that day the collector was late; he was the last person there, I believe; I bid all the men good night when I left, and the boiler saw me out of the gate.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420131-836]
836. CHARLES DICKER was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of June, 1 gelding, price 4 l. 15 s., the property of Thomas Miller; and that he, had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE KING . I am in the employ of Edward Wallis, a horse-slaughterer, in Garratt-lane, Wands worth. On the 16th of June the prisoner came to me with a black horse belonging to Mr. Thomas Miller, of Croydon, who is a sand-man, and keeps a beer-shop. I know his master very well—his Christian name is Thomas—I lent him another horse to go home with as be said he had left his own in our field—he left Miller's black horse in our field, and said to me, "What can you give me for the other horse?" meaning Miller's black one—I told him to tell his master I would allow him 55 s. for it—next morning he came and brought my horse back, saying he would take the money I had bid him—I sealed the money up in a letter, put a note inside it, and gave him 1 s. for himself—he went down towards Wandsworth, and tied his horse, which he had brought with mine, up at a sign-post, and went away with the money—I gave him the money to deliver to his master, believing he had authority to sell the horse—it was the black horse which had been turned out into the field—my master's daughter wrote a note with the money.
THOMAS MILLER . I keep a beer-shop and am a broom-maker; I live at Croydon. On the 17th of June the prisoner left my service—I was not aware of his bargaining for the sale of one of my horses—I never authorized him to sell the black horse—he never brought me the money for it—I had sent him to Wandsworth on the 16th with a cart and two horses, and he brought Wallis's horse home, saying the black horse was knocked up, and appeared ill, and Mr. Wallis bad lent him a horse to bring home, which he must return about ten or eleven next morning—I said, "Well, be punctual, and get there in time"—I tied Wallis's horse behind my cart, and expected him home at night, but Wallis's man came over, and said be had bought the horse—I did not see the prisoner again till he was in custody.
WILLIAM LEE . In consequence of information I received, I went to the shop of Townsend, a clothes-dealer, in High-street, Wandsworth—I saw a note taken out of the pocket of a pair of trowsers there, by the shopman, which I produce—I cannot identify the trowsers—the shopman has left.
WILLIAM REED . I apprehended the prisoner at Brixton-hill—I made Aim no promise, but cautioned him if he said anything, I must state it before the Magistrate—he acknowledged that he had absconded with the money, and hoped if he got over this, he should not do anything more wrong.
GEORGE KING re-examined. The note produced by Lee is what I sent by the prisoner with the money—( read)—"Sir,—I have sent the money for the horse, as agreed on yesterday, 2 l. 15 s., which you will find enclosed."
Prisoner's Defence. The policeman told me I had better tell what I had done with the money, or I should be sorry for it; this was an old horse, which master bought at a sale; it fell down four times, when the cast was empty; when I came to Wallis's with a load of bricks, it fell again; people said I should never get home with it; King said, "As I know your master, I will lend you a horse to go home, and will turn yours into my field;" he told me to tell my master what he would give for the horse; I told my mistress; she said she would sooner have the one I had brought than her own; and in the morning, master said he thought of selling it at Smithfield;
when I got back, master's horse laid dead in the field, and I took the money for it; there was no water in the trough; as I was getting some out of the river, I slipped in up to my middle, with the paper I had the money in; as I went up the lane, I dropped the paper with the money; a cart wheel ran over it, and cut the paper asunder; I had dropped some of the money in the river, and had 30 s. left; I bought a pair of trowsers, and having a bad master, (who even suffered me at night to turn his horses into other farmer's fields, and had kicked me down eight steps a few days before,)I thought I should be made a bigger thief than I am; I never received a farthing of money, and was with him five weeks.
THOMAS MILLER . I said nothing to induce him to say anything.
GEORGE KING re-examined. The hone was not dead when he came in the morning—it was down, and in a dreadfully bad state—it was dying, but it laid the next day and night before it died—there was grass in the meadow where it was—it died in the field—I knew it would not live when be came for the money—its lungs were gone, and it was rotten altogether.
THOMAS MILLER re-examined. I have no reason to know that he said anything about the horse to my wife—he said something of this sort at Wandsworth—I had no idea that he had sold it—I never turn my horses out of a night—I took the prisoner out of charity, and paid him 18 d. a night.
JACOB CALMUT ( police-constable P 190.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from Mr. Lawson, the Clerk of the Peace for Surrey—( read)—I know the prisoner to be the person.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18420131-837]
837. ELLEN DALEY was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of January, 3 pints of gin, value 3 s.; and 2 bottles, value 4 d.; the goods of John Watkins, her master.
JOHN WATKINS . I keep the Goat's-head public-house in the Wandsworth-road—the prisoner was my cook for about three months. On going into my cellar on the 11th of January, I discovered a quantity of gin on the floor under a vat, and found the key of that vat on the top of an adjoining vat, with the handle wet, as if just laid down by a wet hand—I went into the kitchen, and found two bottles of gin in the meat-screen—I placed them in the same situation in which I found them, and with Trimmings, my son-in-law, retired into a little dairy adjoining the kitchen, to watch—Trimmings got on a chair, so as to look over the top of the door, where there is a blind—in a short time the prisoner came into the kitchen, opened the door of the meat-screen, took out the two bottles of gin, placed them under her apron, and was about to take them out of the kitchen-door into the yard—she went across the kitchen to go out—I then came out of the dairy, stopped her at the door, and said, "Mary, these two bottles of gin belong to me"—I took them from her, sent for a policeman, and she was taken into custody—her room was searched, but nothing of mine found.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is there a dresser in the kitchen? A. Yes—I have a pot-boy—the prisoner and he have not had any quarrel to my knowledge—I never heard that the prisoner was called down into the kitchen, with directions that there was a goose to be roasted—she had no notice of her trunks being searched—I had as good a character with her as a girl need to have.
WILLIAM WILSON . I am pot-man to the prosecutor—my master ordered me to wash some wine bottles on the 10th of January—I washed three dozen—I missed two bottles—they were such as these which contain the gin—I cannot swear they are the bottles, but they are similar.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you ever had any quarrel with the prisoner A. No—I swear that—I know nothing of her being called down to roast a goose.
EDWARD WIGGINS . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner, and received these two bottles at the prosecutor's bar.
JOHN WATKINS re-examined. The prisoner said nothing when I stopped her with the bottles, but was very much confused—the outside of the bottles were very wet—I did not discover that her apron was wet—I do not keep my cellar-door open—the prisoner had applied for the key to my son—he is not here.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Williams.
[trialAccount t18420131-838]
838. JAMES ATKINSON, alias John Smith, was indicted for feloniously, and without lawful excuse, being at large within Her Majesty's dominions, before the expiration of the term for which he had been ordered to be transported.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
BARTHOLOMEW HERRING ( police-constable P 187.) I produce a certificate which I received from Mr. Straight, the deputy clerk of Assize—I saw Mr. Straight sign it—it is his signature—I took the prisoner into custody in company with my brother officer, on the 28th of December last, at his father's house, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington, in the county of Surrey—I asked him what his name was—he said, "John Smith"—I told him he was taken for being at large before the end of the term of his sentence for which he was transported—he said, "I was never out of the country in my life"—I searched him, and found on him some files in a box with some lucifers—I saw this handkerchief with him coming along, and also some skeleton keys—( the certificate was here read, stating that the prisoner had been ordered to be transported for life.)
GEORGE GOFF . In December, 1828, I was a constable of St. Mary's, Lambeth—I was at Kingston Assizes that month, and saw the prisoner there—I was present at his trial—another man, named John Wigg, was tried with him—I was in court when sentence of death was ordered to be recorded—I had known him before, and am quite sure he is the man who was then convicted, and sentenced—he was tried before Mr. Baron Vaughan, or Mr. Justice Burrows, I do not know which—they were both there at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Before Sir James Burrows, was it? A. I think it was—I am not certain which it was—I had two cases there, and one was tried before one Judge, and one before the other—one case was for robbing Judge Bay ley's sister, Mrs. Hankey—it was in 1828—I went down on the Saturday as the Assizes began, and they finished on Thursday, the 1st of January—the prisoner is altered—he is older, but there is something about him by which I should know him—he is altered in colour, according to the length of time he has been gone, but not so very much—when I saw him at Union-hall, the officers told me he had been up twice before—if there is anything due to me for this, I expect to get
whatever there is—I know there was formerly an Act of Parliament where there was a reward—I do not know whether there is now—I did not come into office till the rewards were put an end to—I was sworn in in 1817, and left in 1831 or 1832, on account of the new police being established—when I applied for my warrant to be renewed, they told me I was not wanted—I was not a constable of Union-hall, only as an extra-constable to assist the others—I was never on the establishment—I do not know that there was any other reason for my leaving—I had no fault found with me—I was never accused of anything—I gave evidence against a few men for the Mint—my conduct with respect to coin was never investigated to my knowledge—no fault was ever found with me to my face—I did not say when the prisoner was shown to me, that he was so much altered I should not have known him, nor any thing to that effect—that I swear—I am quite positive of him—I have not been inquiring about what money will be coming to me—I swear that—I have not been to the Secretary of State's office to inquire about any reward—I went there about a week or a fortnight ago—there was no quarrel between me and any other person about the way in which we were to get the money.
MR. DOANE. Q. What did you go to the Secretary of State's office for? A. To ask whether there was any account of this man's having deserted, because I would not make any mistake.
ROBERT COLE . I am a watchman in the County jail, at Horsemonger-lane. I remember the prisoner being brought to the jail, in 1828, with John Wigg—I cannot exactly say the date—I remember the Assizes being held at Kingston—it was before the Assizes that he was brought—I saw him go down to the Assizes, and I saw him after the Assizes—I took him on board the Dolphin bulk, at Chatham, with other convicts—I was one of the guards—the prisoner is the man.
Cross-examined. Q. You recollect him perfectly, do you? A. Very well—he looks rather darker, and older than he did in 1828, but there is no difference in his features—I went before the Magistrate on this charge, and stated that I knew him—I did not express the least hesitation, or say that I should not have known him—I knew him him directly I saw him.
RICHARD MYERS . In 1828 I was a constable. I was at the Assizes at Kingston in December that year—I took the prisoner down in a van among a number of others—I had known him for a number of years before—I was present at his trial, and brought him from the Sessions-house to the jail—I have no doubt in the world that he is the man.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you cease to be an officer? A. When the new police came in force, that was the reason—I sent my warrant in—I was a parish-constable—I used not to be employed by the Mint—I used to go there occasionally—there was no investigation about my conduct—I was in business as a tobacconist, but have retired these two years—I have lived in the same house thirty years.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Six Months, and then Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18420131-839]
839. JOHN BENNETT was indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering, on the 12th of January, a forged request for the delivery of goods, with intent to defraud Richard Moser the elder, and another.
JOHN BILL . I am clerk to Richard Moser, senior, and another, ironmerchants,
No. 165, Borough. I know the prisoner, he has come to our firm three or four times for steel—on the 12th of January I saw him there—it is my duty to make out the invoices, which I did for these goods—I do not know that the prisoner spoke to me—he presented me with a written order for the goods now produced—he said nothing—believing it to be genuine, I made out a bill for two bundles of spring-steel, 3 l. 12 s.—I do not remember his saying any thing—he took the steel away—he had had goods before—not on his own account.
Prisoner. I did not have the steel, and never made out the order—I was on board a steam-boat at the time. Witness. I am sure he is the man, I know he went away with the steel—I cannot recollect whether he took it on his shoulder or in a truck—he must have had a truck, it weighed 2cwt. lqr.—I am certain he is the man.
JEMIMA DRY . I am a widow, and carry on the business of a wheelwright and smith, in Horse and Groom-yard, Westminster. The prisoner was in my employ five years ago, as hammerman to his father, who worked for me—I have not seen him since—he was discharged five years ago—I dealt with Messrs. Moser then, and do so now—this order is not my writing—I never authorised any body to write it for me, and none of the goods ever came to my premises.
WILLIAM MORRISS . I am porter to Moser and Son. I know the prisoner, and have seen him at our premises more than three times—I cannot state the date, but I have seen him twice since Christmas—I delivered some steel to him on a Tuesday, the last time he came, but I know nothing about the order now produced—it was another order—I cannot recollect the transaction of the 12th of January—I know nothing of the delivery of these goods myself—we have no other customer named Dry—( order read)—"12th January, 1842. Please let the bearer hare two bundles of spring steel.—J. DRY."
GUILTY . Aged 22.
[trialAccount t18420131-840]
840. JOHN BENNETT was again indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering, on the 27th of January, a forged order for the delivery of goods, with intent to defraud Richard Moser the elder, and another.
WILLIAM MORRISS . I am a yard porter to Messrs. Moser. I know the prisoner by sight—I am positive of him—he brought me this order on the 27th of January, and asked me to look out the steel for him—I said, "Your people are using a great deal of steel, and you tell young master Dry to be more careful in his writing; he is getting a worse scholar now than before he went to school"—the prisoner said young Dry was a bad scholar—I selected the steel in consequence of the order, weighed it, and our yard clerk had it to enter, and gave him the invoice—I am positive he is the man—I delivered the steel to him, helped him tie it up, and he took it away on his shoulder—it was under 56lbs.
JOHN BILL . I am clerk to the prosecutors. On the 27th of January the prisoner came, I ticked this order, and gave him the invoice—it came to 2 l. 11 s. 6 d., weighing 2qrs. 91bs.—be took it away on his shoulder—I noticed the order at the time—it was signed "J. Dry," which Mrs. Dry generally signs.
JEMIMA DRY . This paper is not my writing, nor my son's—I never authorised anybody to write it—my son is not in the habit of drawing these orders, and if he did, he always signed my name as well as his own—I always sign Jemima in full.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a man at the Marsh-gate, who said be would give me 2 s. to go and get this steel, while he went to see somebody down the New-cut; I went and brought him the steel; he gave me 2 s.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Ten Years.
(There were five other indictments against the prisoner.)
Before Mr. Justice Cresswell.
[trialAccount t18420131-841]
841. WILLIAM GINBY and DOROTHY GINBY were indicted for feloniously receiving of a certain evil-disposed person, on the 15th of January, 1 watch, value 10 l.; 1 coat, value 2 l.; 1 waistcoat, value 10 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; 2 toilet covers, value 2 s.; 1 shift, value 2 s.; 1 petticoat, value 3 s.; and 1 shirt, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of James Hunt Burgess; well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
ELIZABETH BURGESS . I am the wife of James Hunt Burgess, a lighterman, of Grange-walk, Bermondsey. On Saturday, the 15th of January, I went out about six o'clock in the evening—nobody was left in the house—the windows and door were fastened—I returned in about an hour and a quarter by myself, unlocked the door, and fell over a candlestick in the passage—I got a light, and found the front-parlour very much disordered—I missed a table-cover and a brown mantle out of the front parlour, and from other parts of the house a gold watch, a coat, a pair of trowsers, a waistcoat, a shirt, seven shifts, two petticoats, three night-gowns, two toilet-covers, one black mantle, and an Orleans cloth dress—on Tuesday, the 18th, I saw them again at the police-office—I had seen them all in the house at six o'clock on Saturday night.
JAMES HUNT BURGESS . On the 15th of January I came home shortly after eight o'clock, and missed the things—I had some bills left at Mr. Marchant's, the pawnbroker—on Monday evening I received a communication from him, went there, and saw my watch—while I was there the female prisoner came in to pledge a coat—I did not bear what she said, nor see what she brought—a policeman was sent for—I went to the station with her, and thence to her residence, with a policeman, (leaving her at the station)—we there found the male prisoner—it is a general dealer's shop—we searched the house—I found up stairs, in a drawer in a bed-room, a shift, a petticoat, a shirt, and a toilet-cover of mine, and on the drawers another toilet-cover—the male prisoner was taken into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you hear the policeman tell him that he had come to search his house, and that his wife was in custody for felony? A. No—he was admitted to bail by the Magistrate, and surrendered to-day.
JOHN HENRY SYMES . I am in the employ of Thomas Marchant, a pawnbroker. On Saturday, the 15th of January, the female prisoner came about eight o'clock in the evening, and pawned a suit of clothes—about an hour after my employer took a gold watch in pawn from her, in my presence—on the 17th we received a printed bill respecting the property stolen, and finding the watch described, I sent our boy to inform Burgess, who came; and while I was showing him the watch, the female prisoner came to pawn a coat and handkerchief—I was told the prisoner was in the shop while I was speaking to Burgess, and I gave her into custody—I left the watch in the care of Mr. Marchant—I have brought it here.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it gold? A. Yes—the female prisoner was dressed much as she is now, with the exception of a bonnet—the watch is worth about 7 l.—she had 2 l. 10 s. on it—30 s. on the 15th, and 1 l. mote on the Monday—the 15th was the first transaction I had with her.
JOHN HUNT . I am a policeman. I took the female prisoner in charge, and took her to the station—I there saw the duplicate of the suit of clothes taken from her by the female searcher—she tried to get it from the searcher—I was outside the door, and heard arguments arising between them—she was resisting its being taken from her, and I took it.
JAMES TIDMARSH . I am a policeman. On the evening of the 17th of January I saw the female prisoner in charge—I knew her before, and where she lived—I went with Burgess to the house, and saw the male prisoner—I told him I had come to search his house, his wife was in custody for felony—he seemed surprised, and said he did not know she was out—I searched the house, and found this property, which the prosecutor identified.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is the Mint in the Borough from where Burgess lives? A. About a mile and a quarter—the male prisoner came up stairs with us, and showed us all over the house—I went to the home three times, and searched every time—the first time was within about seven or eight minutes only—the second might be a quarter of an hour—the property was on the first floor—the prisoner said it was his and his wife's bedroom—there are two more bed-rooms, one occupied by three of the children, and the top room by a brother, who was in bed.
Q. You found him at home each time you went to search? A. Yes—I saw the things the first time I went, and went back for Mr. Burgen—I took some things away the first time, and saw the others there—I took them away the third time—he lighted me up stairs—I did not know what was stolen the first time—I found them in the same place when I went again—the male prisoner is a milkman.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM GINBY— NOT GUILTY .
DOROTHY GINBY— GUILTY . Aged 30.- Confined Twelve Month.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420131-842]
842. HENRY SUMMERSALL was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of December, 1 sovereign, 3 half-sovereigns, 4 crowns, 8 half-crowns, and 30 shillings, the monies of Anthony Ferderer, in the dwelling-house of Rachael Davies.
ANTHONY FERDERER . I am a clock-maker, and live in St. Andrew's row, New Kent-road—I have known the prisoner for some years. On Christmas eve he brought a customer to my house for a clock, and another on the Monday evening—he asked for a Christmas box—I gave him a German silver chain from a drawer, which I opened—the chain caught against a paper containing money, and four or five half-crowns fell out—the prisoner said, "I wish I had got that money"—I said, I wished I bad some more, there was not much—he came again on the Tuesday with another customer—he bad left me half-a-crown on the first clock—I got nothing more—the customers were to pay 1 s. a week for the clocks, and when they could afford it, 2 s.—the first clock was 2 l.—the other two 35 s. each—I was to call on the prisoner's father, where he lodged, for the first instalment—I did not get any thing—he was not at home the first time
—the second time he told me he did not know the people, except he saw them in the street, and I must go and find them out—I scolded him—on going home, on Thursday evening, in consequence of what was said to me by a lodger, I looked into my drawer, and missed the money stated—(I had seen it safe on Wednesday)—it was in the same drawer I had given him the chain from, in the front-parlour—I had rolled all the money up is the same paper on the Wednesday evening, and placed it at the back of the drawer—Hofmire works in that room for himself.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long have you known the prisoner? A. Two years—I have known hit parents nearly seven—I sold a clock to them two years ago—he came to my house occasionally—I let him have the clocks for the people, as I thought it was all right—I knew he lived with his father—I have seen him there many times—I have not dined or drank tea there—I have been in the habit of doing business with his father for even years—I never kept my money-drawer locked—I expected nobody would come to my room—Hofmire went to the room whenever he liked, and so did Rachael Davies, the landlady—there are no servants—I make my own bed—I have two rooms—I keep my parlour-door locked, and put the key in the landlady's kitchen—I had seen the prisoner on the Thursday morning—be never came to my house before Christmas eve, and three times after—his father has had two clocks and a watch since I have known him.
JOSEPH HOFMIRE . I am a clock-maker, and live with Ferderer—we occupy the same room to work in, and pay the rent of the front-parlour between us—I have a bed-room up stairs—I was there the second time the prisoner came, when he asked for a Christmas-box, and heard him say be should like the money very much—on the "Thursday I met him at the end of St. Andrew's-row, about half-past four o'clock—he said, "How do you do?"—I said, "Very well; how is it you have been over here?"—he said, "I want to see Mr. Ferderer very particularly, about them clocks he sold"—I said, "You have been in our place"—he said, "Yes, Mr. Ferderer is not at home, I will go back with you and wait till he comes home"—the lodger up stairs let us in—I went in, fetched the key from the kitchen, and let him into the workshop—I found the door locked—I lighted the fire, and the prisoner sat down close to the prosecutor's drawer—I knew there was money there—I put the kettle on the fire and began working—a woman in the next room called out to me, "I have got a good fire, if you want to put your kettle on"—so I went into the other room and put it on—I then went up stairs, leaving the prisoner in the work-room—I came down in a few minutes, and found the prisoner at the street-door, ready to go out—I said, "Are you not going to stop till Mr. Ferderer comes home?"—he said, "No, I had rather go to a public-house and have a pint of beer and smoke a pipe, and be back in half an hour"—I said, "If you want some beer I will get you some, you can smoke a pipe here, or you can have tea with me"—he said he had rather go to a public-house, and be back in half an hour—he left about five o'clock, and did not return—Ferderer came in about half-past five—in consequence of what I told him he went to his drawer and missed his money—I had seen the paper containing the money that morning, when I took a gimlet out of the drawer, after Ferderer went out—it had the usual appearance of having money in it—when I went out I shut and locked the workshop—there is no door opens from the workshop to the other room—you must go out into the passage—
the woman had seen me come in—when J went out I had hung the key on a nail in the kitchen, which I usually do—the prisoner did not call out to me—when I came down he had the latch of the street-door in his band to go out.
Cross-examined. Q. He stood talking to you in the hall, awhile, did he not? A. Yes—the street-door was not kept open, but any body could pull it open—when I saw the paper in the morning, I saw that it had something rolled in it—I had seen the paper the Wednesday before, when he took the chain out—it was in the back part of the drawer—I pulled the drawer about half out to get the gimlet, which was in the middle of it—the drawer is only about half a foot long, and half a foot wide—I cannot think of the name of the lodger who let us in—it was not the landlady—it was the woman up stairs—( looking at his depositions)—this is my name and handwriting—(the witness's depositions being read, stated "the landlady then opened the street-door to us ")—I did say before the Magistrate that the landlady let us in—I forgot it—it was three weeks afterwards.
JURY. Q. Was it the same person who let you in that offered to boil your kettle on her fire? A. No—that was the woman in the back parlour—I do not know whether she spoke from the passage or her room—there are two lodgers, both married women and their husbands live in the house.
RACHAEL DAVIES . I am a widow, and lire in Clarence-terrace, St. Andrew's-row. I am the landlady of the house, and the witnesses lodge there, and two others—one of them has two rooms up stairs, and one down stairs—they are married—Mr. and Mrs. Sherwin lodge in the two rooms up stairs, and a man and his wife in the back-room—I cannot speak the name—the two witnesses have the front workshop, and a room up stairs—I have no servant—when the two witnesses go out, they lock their door and bring the key to me—I put it into my room, which we call the kitchen, and keep it till they come back at night—nobody could get at it when I was at home—when I was out they might—on the Thursday I was at home all day—I was at home about five o'clock—I had not been out at all—I work in the kitchen when I am at home—I go in and out to the yard—I do not take the key with me—I never lock the kitchen door—the key was safe all day—Ferderer went out between eight and nine in the morning, and Hofmire directly after—I was in the kitchen, as near as I can say, from nine to five—pretty nearly all that time—I was in the kitchen when Hofmire came in for the key—as far as I know, nobody had had it since he went out in the morning—nobody could get into the workshop without my hearing them—it is a small house—the key was left in my care—I do not know who let Hofmire in at five—I did not—the lady up stairs did, as they say—the two female lodgers were at home at five—I heard the knock at the door, and one of them let him in—I cannot say who it was.
Cross-examined. Q. When were you examined? A. This day week—I was not at the police-office when Hofmire was examined—when he came home from the office they told me I had let him in, and I said it was a mistake—Ferderer did not say so, it was one Joseph—the other lodgers never lock their doors—one of them is a porter at Charing-cross—the other was out of work, and had been for some time—he belonged to a steam-vessel—I was in my own room pretty nearly all day—I did not stay in it entirely—I cannot recollect how often I went into
the yard—I was washing in the kitchen, and I hung the things in the kitchen to dry—I do not think I went a dozen times into the yard—I will not swear it—there is only one flight of stairs in the house—one lodger lives below and one above—the two witnesses have the other room up stairs—I never saw the prisoner there—the key hung on a peg in the kitchen, and I mostly hang a cloth over it, but not always, or a shawl or a bonnet—I never missed the key from the time it was given me till he came for it—the Magistrate did not require the other lodgers to attend—the one that let Hofmire and the prisoner in, went with me, and the Magistrate said she was not wanted.
JOHN BARKER ( police-constable K 108.) I took the prisoner into custody on the 20tb of January, in Whitechapel-road—I told him he was wanted at Mile-end station—he said very well, he would go—I said it was respecting some money over the water—he said he knew nothing about it—on going a little further, he said he bad heard of it, but knew nothing about it.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
