846. WILLIAM FEWKES was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of January, 5 sovereigns, 2 half-sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 3 shillings, the monies of John Fewkes: and HENRY CONNORTON , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN FEWKES . I keep a shop in John-street, Waterloo-road, Lambeth—William Fewkes is my son—he absented himself from me on the 6th of January, and I lost five sovereigns, two half-sovereings, two half-crowns, and three shillings, from a box in my kitchen—my three girls slept in the bed in the kitchen, and the prisoner slept at the foot of it in a chair—the box was broken open—it had been safe at eleven o'clock the night before—I gave notice at the police-station—I went to Greenwich, and when I came back, a boy told me that my son and Connorton had been seen together—I went to Connorton's father on the Sunday morning after—I Connorton there—he said he had seen my boy, and that he had 6 l. 8 s.—I said, "Did he tell you where he got it?"—he said, "Yes, he said he took it from you"—I said, "Where did you get those new boots you have on?"—he said, "I bought them, and I got the money honestly"—I gave him in charge—he told the inspector that this was all he bad out of the money—the Magistrate could not commit him till I took my son, and when I found my son, I took Connorton again.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When you saw Connorton first, you told him he had better tell you all about it? A. I did not—I asked him if he had seen my boy, and what money he had with him—I never told him that my boy had been robbing me—I never saw him till I took him—I did not tell him I was the father of Fewkes—he must have known me, because he bad been whistling at nights after my boy—my lodger has seen him do so.
COURT. Q. You missed this money? A. Yes, and have not found any of it—I have had no conversation with my son—when he was at Union-hall he would not speak to me—nobody but my son could have taken this money—it is impossible, as they must have pulled him out of bed to get to the box—he slept nearly on the box, and he set off that morning at seven o'clock—his mother was not dressed.
GEORGE BEATTY ( police-constable L 80.) I took the prisoner Fewkes—he said he gave Connorton half the money—they would not acknowledge it till they went before the Magistrate—there they confessed it—Connorton acknowledged that the boots were bought for him out of the money—Fewkes made a statement before the Magistrate—I know Mr. Traill's handwriting—this is his writing to this deposition.
Cross-examined. Q. Who is Mr. Traill? A. The Magistrate at Unionhall—I was at the office that day, and I think I have been there three or four times before, or oftener—I have been three years in the police—I do not remember how often I had been before Mr. Traill before this case—I had been once before—that was about a man who stole a cheese—I think that was twelve months ago or better—I think I saw Mr. Traill write on that occasion—I am not quite sure—I have seen him write more than once, but I do not remember when—I have seen him write on other cases as well as my own—I saw him write on the 25th of January—he was sitting on the bench where he always sits—I was standing nearly opposite to him—I cannot tell what he wrote—I saw him write, and it was handed into my own hand afterwards—I saw the certificate of the notice I got, which he signed—I have it here—I have seen him write several times before—his Christian name is James—I am sure about that—( read)—"The prisoner Fewkes says, I confess to taking the money. The prisoner Connorton I went with Fewkes and bought a pilot-coat, and after that, he divided says, his money with me; I had two sovereigns in gold, and some other money."
FEWKES- GUILTY . Aged 12. CONNORTON- GUILTY. Aged 12.
Confined six months.
[trialAccount t18420131-847]
847. RICHARD LIGHT was indicted for bigamy.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
SUSAN HUGHES . In 1823, I lived in the neighbourhood of Shrewsbury—I knew Mr. Eaton, of Claremont-buildings, there—the prisoner was in Mr. Eaton's service as footman—I remember in 1823, there was a young woman with whom he kept company, Emma Maria Fox—I knew her—the prisoner asked me to go to church with him and Emma Fox—I said, "I suppose you are going to be married?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, Ann Davis would rather go (Ann Davis is now dead) I did not go—the prisoner was often talking of his marriage, and so was Ann Davis too
—they were married at the church of St. Chad, at Shrewsbury—the prisoner told me so—he said the minister's name was Mr. Sandford—they afterwards went to live at her brother's, Richard Fox's, in New-street—they lived together as man and wife—I saw them together after, and they have been at our house together—I left the prisoner at Mr. Eaton's—I saw Emma Fox after the prisoner said he was going to church-—I saw her wedding-ring on the fourth finger—they lived together in Edom-place—I saw Emma Fox alive last Thursday four weeks, at Manchester.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you know how long they lived together? A. No—I saw Emma Fox at Manchester—she was living with her husband, whose name is Bell—she is married now—I went to see whether she was alive—Mr. Barnes sent me—he is an architect—she said she was very comfortable.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know whether she is married to Bell? A. No.
WILLIAM BARNES . I produce a copy of the register of marriage from St. Chad's, Shrewsbury—I examined it myself with the register of marriages—I am related by marriage to Margaret Turner Thompson—I prosecute this case—I took this certificate down to Shrewsbury by order of Mr. Traill, and found the parish clerk—the register was open—I compared this copy with the book, and Mr. Webster did the same, and signed it—I will swear it is a true copy—( reads)—"Richard Light, of this parish, bachelor, and Emma Fox, of this parish, spinster, were married in this church by banns, this 16th of July, 1823, by me, Thomas Landford, minister. " WILLIAM SPEAK,"
In the presence of " ANN DAVIS. "
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Before you had obtained this, had you seen the prisoner? A. Yes, on the 3rd of August, at my house—he made a statement to me, which I took down in writing at the time—he said he was married at St. Chad's on the 16th of July, 1823; that he married Emma Fox, a dress-maker, by whom he had three children, and one was dead; that he left his wife and two children in Manchester, that be had agreed to leave them, and never see them again—he stated that William Speak and Ann Davis were witnesses to the marriage, and that the clergyman was Mr. Landford.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you made him any promise that he should not be prosecuted? A. No—I swear he made the statement without my giving him any inducement—I told him I should make use of the evidence against him—his third wife, Butler, was present.
GEORGE YARROW . I am parish clerk of Shoreditch. I produce the register of marriages for that parish—I have the entry of a marriage of Richard Light with Margaret Turner Thompson—( read)—"Marriage solemnized in the parish of Shoreditch, No. 268, 23rd November, 1837. Richard Light and Margaret Turner Thompson, both full age, bachelor and spinster, servants; Light living in New North-street, and Thompson in Well-street; and signed by them in presence of George Yarrow and Jemima Trance."
MARGARET TURNER THOMPSON . I have known the prisoner since 1832—I went through the ceremony of marriage with him at Shoreditch church, on the 23rd of November, 1837—we were married by banns—he
described himself as a single man—he lived two years and a fortnight with me, and then left me.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you known him? A. Since 1832—he lived at Crosby, five miles from Liverpool—he was living with Mrs. Luce, in George-square, Liverpool—we never lived in the same house together before we were married—I have lived in James-street, Buckingham gate—I was seduced by him, and clung to him till he married me—we lived together for two years before we were married—I lived with him because he promised me marriage.
GEORGE WARNER ( City police-constable, No 325.) I took the prisoner on the 12th of December, in Lombard-court, Fleet-street—I found him on a roof of a house behind a stack of chimnies—I said I must take him—he said, "No, don't; go back, and say you can't find me"—I said I had been looking after him some time, and it was more than I dare do—I had a warrant to take him—he said, "I suppose it is about Margaret Turner's marriage?"—I said, "Yes, you must go with me"—he still refused to come off the roof, and after persuading him some time I got hold of him by the collar—he said, sooner than he would go he would rather throw us both down, and break our necks—the distance was from thirty to forty feet—after some persuasion I succeeded in taking him.
Cross-examined. Q. Could he not have brought you both down? A. It would not have taken a great deal of trouble—he was alone on the roof.
GUILTY . Aged 42.
[trialAccount t18420131-848]
848. RICHARD LIGHT was again indicted for bigamy.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
SUSAN HUGHES . I was in Shrewsbury in 1832—I knew the prisoner at Mr. Eaton's—the prisoner asked me to go to church—I said, "Ann Davis would rather go"—he was often talking of that marriage—the account I gave in the last case is correct.
WILLIAM BARNES . I produce a copy of the marriage register, and it is correct—the prisoner called on me, and said he was married at St. Chad's, on the 16th of July, 1823, to Emma Fox, a dress-maker, by whom he had three children, that one was dead, and that he left his wife and two children in Manchester.
GEORGE TURNER . I am parish clerk of St. Peter, Walworth, in St. Mary, Newington. I have the marriage register, in which there is an entry that on the "18th February, 1840, (No. 284) Richard Light and Hannah Butlin, of full age, widower and spinster, by trade a grocer, were married in this church. (Witness)
" GEORGE TURNER, ANN BAILEY."
ANN BAILBY . I live in Upper Thames-street. I was present at the prisoner's marriage in 1840—he was married to Hannah Butlin—they lived at our house after that—they might have lived two or three months there.
COURT. Q. How old was she? A. I cannot say—I believe about twenty-five.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know her very well? A. I knew her when she came to our house to lodge—she was a servant—I have not
heard that the prisoner got any money with her-—I have not seen her since she left our house, which is nearly two years.
GUILTY . Aged 42.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420131-849]
849. ISAAC LINTON was indicted for embezzlement.
JAMES PAGE . I live at Merton—the prisoner was my carman. On the 28th of September I sent him to Mr. Evans's with a ton of potatoes and half a load of straw, which came to 2 l. 9 s.—I called some time after, and Mr. Evans was not at home—I then sent the prisoner—if he had received that money it was his duty to pay it me immediately—he has not paid it me.
DANIEL EVANS . I am a cow-keeper, and live in Dorset-square. On the 28th of September I paid the prisoner 2 l. 9 s. for Mr. Page
Prisoner. I put it into a purse with some of my own, 7 l. 5 s. in all; I lost it out of my pocket.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Justice William.
[trialAccount t18420131-850]
850. THOMAS EGGLETON was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of September, 671bs. weight of lead, value 12 s., belonging to the inhabitants of the county of Surrey, and fixed to a building.—2nd COUNT, the same not stating it to be fixed.—3rd COUNT, stating it to belong to Henry Heathcote Russell.
HENRY HEATHCOTE RUSSELL . I live in Freeman's-court, Cornhill. I am clerk of the works, and superintendent of the Lunatic Asylum for the county of Surrey—there was some lead missing about the 21st or 22nd of September, 1839—it was a portion of the ridge lead, gone from a tool-house in the kitchen garden—it had been cut in length, and taken away—about half a cwt. was left—the policeman brought the lead, in a bag on the 23rd of September—I compared it—seven pieces had been cut, which were worth 10 s. or 12 s.—five out of the seven were brought back—the prisoner had been employed as a labourer there, I believe during the month of September—the tool-house belongs to the Asylum—it was formerly the residence of Mr. Perkins, and the Magistrates of Surrey purchased the estate to build the Asylum.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What were the works going on? A. Building the Asylum—Mr. Edward Lappenter, the architect, employed me—I lived there, in Mr. Perkins's house—I had the whole charge of the property.
COURT. Q. Had Mr. Perkins any thing to do with it during the time you were there? A. No.
JOHN WAYMARK ( police-constable of Wandsworth.) I have known the prisoner twenty years—about eight o'clock on the 20th of September, 1839, I passed the prisoner in Garratt-lane, Wanda worth, about half a mile from where the lead was stolen—he had the bag produced with him—he turned up the lane to the Asylum—I did not speak to him—I am sure he is the man.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it was the 20th of September? A. I am sure of it—I saw the bag, soon after it was found in Mr. Martin's fields, with the lead in it—I was about two yards from the prisoner when I saw him in Garratt-lane—it was a light-looking bag, and he was carrying it under his arm—I did not tell the Magistrate that I met him with a white
bag which was quite empty, under his arm, and not the bag which has got the lead in it now.
COURT. Q. Did you notice the bag? A. Yes, under his arm—it was a coarse-looking light bag—there is no mark on it—this is the same sort of bag.
WILLIAM HEATH . I am bailiff of Mr. Martin. I was so in 1839—Garratt-lane parts the Asylum from Mr. Martin's land—Mr. Perkins parted with the land in 1838—he had nothing to do with it after the Asylum had begun building—I had not seen him interfering—he used to do so before—in September, 1839, I was in one of our fields adjoining the lane with some men and women picking up potatoes—it was twenty minutes to nine o'clock, on the 20th of September—Mr. Heath's factory-bell had rung half-past eight—I saw the prisoner come over the stile into the lane into our premises—he had a bag on his back—he ran into the first field, and threw the bag and its contents into a ditch, and covered it with some nettles—I was about thirty yards from him—I went towards him, and met him face to face—I said, "What have you been putting in the ditch?"—he said, "It don't belong to me"—I said, "I suppose it is something you have been stealing; I suppose you have robbed some poor man's house? let us go and see"—we were going to the ditch—I got a little before him—he slipped behind me, and ran away—I sent a boy after a policeman, who came before the boy got back—there was a bag under the nettles with something it it.
Cross-examined. Q. Of course you told the Magistrate all this? A. I did, what I said was taken down, and read over to me, and I signed it—I had never seen the prisoner before that day—I swear to him because I took such particular notice of him—I have said I did not know, till I saw him again whether I should be able to swear to him or not.
COURT. Q. But when you did see him, you knew him? A. I knew him immediately—I have no doubt about its being him—there were three women, a boy, and two men in the field.
EDMUND BRUCE ( police-constable V 19.) On Friday, the 20th of September, 1839, I went with Heath to a ditch, and found a sort of fustian, light bag containing five pieces of lead—I was present when Mr. Russell applied them to the roof of the house—they agreed with the marks where the lead had been ripped from—there were seven pieces taken away—I searched for the prisoner—I could not find him—I did not know where he lived—I searched at Merton, Beddington, and round the country—the building is in the county of Surrey.
( The witness Heath's depositions being read, omitted to state that he had spoken to the prisoner.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18420131-851]
851. JOHN FOX was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of January, 4lbs. of bread, value 10 d., the goods of Francis Turner.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of John Barrett.
JOHN BARRETT . I am journeyman to Francis Turner, a baker, at Clapham-rise. I was delivering bread in Portman-place, Clapham-road, on the 17th of January—I left my barrow, and went down into a kitchen—I saw the prisoner going away—I came up, and ordered him to be taken
to the station, but there was no policeman to be found—he had two of my loaves.
JAMES SMITH . I am a stonemason, and live in Ebenezer-place, Clapham—I saw the prisoner take two loaves out of the barrow—I collared him—there was another with him, but he did not take any thing—he walked away.
Prisoner's Defence. I was looking for work—I met a young lad whom I knew by sight—he went to the barrow, and took these two loaves, brought them to me, and said, "Take them"—I said I would take them back to the barrow.
GUILTY *. Aged 17.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18420131-852]
852. EDWARD HATTON was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Dorrell, about the hour of five o'clock in the night, on the 31st of January, at St. Saviour, Southwark, with intent to steal.
JOHN DORRELL . I am a dealer in marine-stores, living in Redcross-street, in the parish of St. Saviour, Southwark—I have purchased things of the prisoner. Last Monday night I shut up at ten o'clock all safe—my wife and daughter and myself sleep in the house—my son and a friend sat up to watch—I came down about five o'clock on Tuesday morning—I thought it was six—I told my son it was six, and he might leave—he said it was only five—I went to bed again—I had not gone five minutes be. fore this happened—it was a quarter-past five—I had lost money before this, and could not account how it had gone.
RICHARD BROWN . I am a warehouseman, living in Cross-lane—I sat up two or three nights at this house. Between five and six o'clock on Tuesday morning, I saw the prisoner get over the wall, which is ten feet high, and lower himself down into the back-yard—he then moved a tub which covered a grating, and lifted up the grating, and dropped himself down into the cellar—a friend, who was with me, let the grating down—we took a light and found the prisoner—he had gone from the back-cellar to the front-cellar, and had got his shoes off ready to come up Stairs—there is a way up from there.
FREDERICK LONG ( police-constable M 100.) I took the prisoner.
(William Hatton, a labourer, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420131-853]
853. CHARLOTTE PENNYFATHER was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of July, 3 sheets, value 6 s.; 1 table-cloth, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 shirt, value 2 s.; 3 waistcoats, value 6 s. 6 d.; 1 coat, value 8 s.; and 12 yards of ticking, value 6 s.; the goods of John Rye, her master.
JOHN RYE . I am a coachman, in the service of Mr. Sutton, of Dulwich—the prisoner was my housekeeper—she had no right to dispose of, or to pawn any of my property. On the 11th of January I missed these things produced, which are all mine—the prisoner absconded that day without notice.
RICHARD LAW . I am a pawnbroker. I have three sheets, two waistcoats, and one table-cloth—they were pawned by the prisoner at various times.
JOHN BRAGG . I am a pawnbroker. I have a bed-tick, a coat, and pair of trowsers pawned by the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I owed a little money. I intended to get them out again. I gave the tickets up when I saw the prosecutor.
GUILTY . Aged 57.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18420131-854]
854. JAMES ELLIOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of January, 7lbs, weight of suet, value 3 s., the goods of John Vooght, his master: and SARAH SAUNDERS , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
ALFRED CAMP . I am apprenticed to John Vooght, a butcher, in Larkhall-lane, Clapham. About half-past eight o'clock, on the 26th of January, I went into a timber-yard adjoining our premises, and saw a bundle of fat tied up in a handkerchief—I took it in, and my mistress opened it—I took it back, and put it into the same place—I believe it to be my master's fat—on my afterwards going to the spot it was gone—I fetched my master—he told me to watch in one place while he watched in another, and at a quarter-past nine he called to me—he said he had got the prisoner—I had seen the prisoner wearing such a handkerchief as the fat was in, round his neck that morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You did not see him do any thing with it? A. No.
JOHN VOOGHT . The prisoner Elliott was in my service. On the night of the 26th of January I was out—my apprentice Camp came to me—in consequence of what he said I told him to watch—I saw the prisoner coming home—I asked him where he had been—he told me—I asked him what be had taken there—he said, "Nothing"—this is mutton suet.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you know Saunders? A. Yes—she is married—I had her husband taken up—we went before the Grand Jury, and they threw out the bill—she has just surrendered to take her trial—I went to Saunders, and found her husband at home.
GEORGE BECK ( police-sergeant V.) I took Elliott—on conveying him to the station he said, "Oh, dear, will my master come?"—he said he hoped he would let him go, and he should not have taken it, if Mrs. Saunders had not come and asked him to give her a piece of fat—when I got to the station I took this handkerchief from Elliott—Saunders said, "This is the handkerchief he brought the fat in to me, and the basket is mine"—this is the basket and fat—I got them from Mr. Vooght.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where did you take Elliott? A. At his master's shop—it was in the absence of Saunders that he told me she had asked him for the fat.
JOSEPH BIGG ( police-sergeant V 8.) I went with the prosecutor to Saunders's house—she denied at first that Elliott had been there—I commenced searching, and then she said to Mr. Vooght, "He has been here, and brought a little fat," and at the same time she gave him the basket, and the fat in it.
Cross-examined. Q. You mentioned to her that a boy had been there? A. Mr. Vooght asked her, and mentioned his name, and she said he had been there—her husband was in the room all the time—I went back and took him—the Magistrate admitted Saunders to bail, and she has surrendered.
(Elliott received a good character.)
ELLIOTT— GUILTY . Aged 16.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury. — Confined Ten Days.
SAUNDERS— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420131-855]
855. HENRY STRIPE was indicted for embezzlement.
HENRY PHILPOTT . I live at Fulham, and am a publican. The prisoner was my pot-boy—it was his duty to pay me the money he received—if he received 1 s. on the 3rd of January, or 6 d. on the 4th, or 1 s. on the 9tb, he has not paid it to me.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. How long had he been with you? A. Nine or ten months—I sent bills round, and by that means discovered the deficiency—there are other deficiencies—I sent him round with beer too or three times a-day—he ought to have paid me when he returned—I did not know every one that he sold beer to, but Mr. Stocking had his beer every day, and Mrs. Shinnick—the prisoner settled with me three or four times a day, sometimes, and sometimes not once in a week—he used to make excuses that people had not paid him—it was his duty to settle with me every time he came back.
FREDERICK JAMES STOCKING . I had beer of the prosecutor—I paid the prisoner every time he came—I paid him on the 3rd of January, 1 s., and on the 4th, 6 d.
Cross-examined. Q. Did it never happen that instead of paying him in the morning, you paid him in the afternoon? A. I paid him at one o'clock at noon, and at nine At night—a bill of 16 s. 9 d. was sent in to me, bat I had paid it all—I do sot think it has ever happened that I have paid the prisoner for the one o'clock beer at night—my wife has sometimes paid him—I know I paid him 1 s. on the 3rd of January, and 6 d. of it was for the Sunday's beer, when we do not pay, and that I paid at nine in the morning.
MARGARET SHINNICK . On the 9th of January I paid the prisoner 1 s.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it was the 9th? 2 l."My mistress told me so—she was in the kitchen—I paid the 1 s.—it was for Saturday and Sunday's beer, and it was on Sunday night I paid it—my mistress is always in the habit of paying regularly—a bill of 2 l. 11 s. 11 d. was sent to my mistress, but I knew it was paid—I saw the bill, and had it in my hand.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Six Months.
ADJOURNED TO FEBRUARY 28 TH.
